TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* The Real Danger of Big Tech
  Foreign Policy | 23 Oct 19:51 UTC
  The biggest risks of AI and other advanced technologies have less to do with their growing power than the men behind them.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For at least a year now, newspapers have been increasingly filled with urgent and anxious reports about the quickening pace of artificial intelligence development, warning ominously that the technology will eventually defy human control and take over the world. It is a threat worth taking seriously, and no mere hype. But with all the attention devoted to AI’s growing power, an even more imminent danger has become clear: With their prodigious fortunes, a small cast of men who lead the world’s most powerful AI-related companies have come to wield extraordinary and perhaps already-unstoppable power over U.S. government and society—and, thereby, unprecedented influence over world affairs. For at least a year now, newspapers have been increasingly filled with urgent and anxious reports about the quickening pace of artificial intelligence development, warning ominously that the technology will eventually defy human control and take over the world. It is a threat worth taking seriously, and no mere hype. But with all the attention devoted to AI’s growing power, an even more imminent danger has become clear: With their prodigious fortunes, a small cast of men who lead the world’s most powerful AI-related companies have come to wield extraordinary and perhaps already-unstoppable power over U.S. government and society—and, thereby, unprecedented influence over world affairs. The richest among them, Elon Musk, is the most spectacular example. He has exhibited no modesty about his ambitions as he has used his Midas-like wealth and tentacular corporations to become an irresistible force in contemporary affairs. The U.S. government now openly depends on Musk, who became a trillionaire in June, for its space program, for global communications, and for holding the country’s own in competition with China in frontier industries such as electric vehicles and batteries. But that is far from all. Early in U.S. President Donald Trump’s second term, the White House accorded a leading role to Musk in shaping—and ostensibly streamlining—the country’s bureaucracy. It gave him not only unprecedented say over large-scale firings and the shuttering of long-standing government entities but also access to the personal data of millions of citizens.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/23/musk-starlink-big-tech-dangers-ultrarich-us-democracy/

In Pawel Pawlikowski’s spare, deeply-felt new film Fatherland , the great novelist Thomas Mann (Hanns Zischler) returns to Germany from his Californian exile, and travels with his daughter Erika (Sandra Hüller) through their ruined, occupied homeland. Toward the end, they walk into a bomb-damaged church, and sit listening as two men play Bach’s “Jesu, Joy of Man’s Desiring” on the organ. Mann weeps for all that is lost. Pawlikowski has picked his moment well. That conference played a key part in building a new global settlement that aimed to ensure, through guaranteed rights and freedoms, through mutual trade and prosperity, that such a catastrophe could never happen again. But today, that postwar order is coming under ever heavier bombardment. Predictions of its destruction are commonplace. It’s a good time to look through the lens of the 1940s, and contemplate just why that order was created in the first place. Back then, Hollywood was filming in the ruins for real. Between 1947 and 1949, four great directors each went to Germany and shot an American spin on what the postwar Germans called the Trümmerfilm , or “rubble movie.” The 1940s was perhaps Hollywood’s apotheosis. The war fostered new depth and seriousness; television had not yet weakened cinema’s dominion. This was the era of Humphrey Bogart, Ava Gardner, and film noir; of movies like Casablanca , Double Indemnity , and Notorious . The four rubble films themselves are hardly classics, but between them, they captured how a newly super-powerful United States was trying to understand how the world should be rebuilt. They dramatized the dilemmas and nightmares Americans faced as they decided whether to entangle themselves with Europe in the long term. Today, they remind us that the creati
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/23/fatherland-nazis-postwar-trauma-rubble-movies/

* How to Make a Monster
  Foreign Policy | 23 Oct 18:00 UTC
  What a novel about Ireland’s last great auks reveals about xenophobia today.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Around 1733, a former manservant to a president of London’s Royal Society sold his hand-illustrated compendium, A Short History of Human Prodigies, and Monstrous Births , to the Society’s current president; one memorable entry was titled “A Wild and Hairy Irishman.” Fifty years later, a Scottish surgeon and anatomist harvested the skeleton of Charles Byrne, known as the Irish Giant, which was displayed in the Royal College of Surgeons of England until 2018. Since classical antiquity, Western travelers, geographers, and naturalists have often described atypical individuals—and distant peoples, real and imagined—as monsters. Medieval maps depicted giants, human-animal hybrids, and figures gnawing on human limbs inhabiting the edge of the world, where harsh climates supposedly gave rise to brutish species. Centuries later, when explorers and colonial administrators wielded European science to serve empire building, perceptions of the Irish paralleled earlier commentators’ descriptions of “wild” and “monstrous” peoples. With U.S. President Donald Trump’s approval rating at record lows amid high inflation spiked by an unpopular war, many pundits view the upcoming midterm elections as the only serious emergency brake left for the nation. Rather than rallying around a common direction—other than opposition to Trump—the Democratic Party appears to lack anything like a coherent governing philosophy or compelling leadership. On the contrary, even on the verge of victory on Nov. 3, Democrats seem to be coming apart at the seams ideologically, making a party that is fractious in the best of times much more so today. It is racked by deep fissures between moderates and candidates allied with left-wing populist forces such as the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), including major divisions over taxes, health care, and Israel. In a bruising Senate primary race in Michigan, for example, most establishment Democrats backed Rep. Haley Stevens, a moderate. The DSA-aligned candidate, Abdul El-Sayed, a public health official who ran on a progressive platform of Medicare for All and major new taxes on the rich, barely squeaked out a win. That costly battle, along with El-Sayed’s controversial criticism of Israel, has dampened his support among centrist voters in a tightening race against former Republican Rep. Mike Rogers. The transnational political firestorm over Azerbaijan’s school hijab ban has produced an extraordinary spat between the governments of Azerbaijan and Turkey, culminating in lurid allegations of Turkish coup-plotting in Azerbaijani state-backed media. These two states have proclaimed themselves the closest of allies—“two states, one nation,” as the popular saying goes. Yet their strong bilateral ties are relatively recent and widely misunderstood. The two countries’ intimacy has created increasing friction as Azerbaijani leaders realize that Turkey’s governing elite view Azerbaijan as less than a co-equal sovereign state. The current round of tensions began on Sept. 21, when the Azerbaijani government approved a more restrictive protocol on school uniforms that effectively banned students from wearing the hijab in the country’s state schools. * Bolivia and Chile Make Nice

  The two countries are mending ties after a decades-long territorial dispute.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Latin America Brief. The highlights this week: Bolivia and Chile mend ties with a historic state visit, hundreds of U.S. troops reportedly deploy to the region, and Meta is accused of favoring far-right posts in Brazil’s presidential campaign . Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Latin America Brief. The highlights this week: Bolivia and Chile mend ties with a historic state visit, hundreds of U.S. troops reportedly deploy to the region, and Meta is accused of favoring far-right posts in Brazil’s presidential campaign . Last week featured a historic moment in South American politics: The presidents of Bolivia and Chile held a state visit for the first time in more than 70 years. The two neighbors have had a territorial dispute for more than a century and severed official diplomatic ties in 1978, maintaining only consular relations since then. In the Bolivian capital of La Paz on Oct. 16, Chilean President José Antonio Kast signed several new agreements with Bolivian President Rodrigo Paz . Kast said he pledged to work “step by step … toward the full normalization of our relationship.” Mining and critical minerals are set to form a key pillar of cooperation. The two countries have been at odds since the War of the Pacific in the late 19th century, which ended with Chile gaining a chunk of land with ocean access that had previously belonged to Bolivia. Relations began to thaw in the past few years following a pair of international court decisions on the matter and the arrival of a moderate-left president in Bolivia in 2020, Luis Arce. Arce’s hard-left predecessor, Evo Morales, had dug in his heels on the territorial dispute. But Arce was open to overtures from the leftist Chilean government of President Gabriel Boric, which worked to gradually collaborate on issues such as fighting crime in the border region, said Andrés Villar, who served as an official in Boric’s Foreign Ministry. Today, both Bolivia and Chile have right-wing presidents, which makes rapprochement even easier. The leaders signed multiple agreements in La Paz last week. An ex-consul general for Chile in Bolivia told La Tercera that it was likely that full diplomatic ties could be restored before the end of Paz’s term in 2030. Paz, for his part, said that landlocked Bolivia was not abandoning its demand for access to the ocean. But “more than our history, what’s important today is to resolve the present and future of our people,” he said. Paz accepted an invita
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/23/bolivia-chile-relations-thaw-kast-paz-lithium/

* Chart: A record two-thirds of car sales in China are now electric vehicles
  Carbon Brief | 23 Oct 15:55 UTC
  For the first time, new-energy vehicles (NEVs) made up 67% of new car sales in China in September, according to… The post Chart: A record two-thirds of car sales in China are now electric vehicles appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: For the first time, new-energy vehicles (NEVs) made up 67% of new car sales in China in September, according to the latest data from the China Passenger Car Association (CPCA). The figures show that NEVs, which include battery electric cars and plug-in hybrids, captured a record share of sales, even though fewer were sold than last year. This is because sales of combustion-engine cars are falling steeply, a trend that the CPCA has attributed to rising petrol prices following the start of the Hormuz crisis . Indeed, sales of fuel-only cars were down 31% year-on-year in the first nine months of 2026, with plug-in hybrid sales down 26% – whereas battery EV sales were only down 4%. One analyst tells Carbon Brief that China’s rapidly growing EV fleet has offset demand for 143m barrels of oil since the start of the Hormuz crisis. Another says that the crisis is “reinforcing China’s electrification of road transport”. Meanwhile, amid slowing domestic sales, Chinese carmakers’ exports of NEVs more than doubled [in 2026 to date / in September] from a year earlier. A record two out of every three cars sold in September in China were NEVs, according to CPCA data, as shown in the figure below. (NEVs predominantly include battery electric vehicles – BEVs – and plug-in hybrids – PHEVs. BEVs only run on electricity, whereas PHEVs also have a combustion engine.) This marks a rapid rise for NEVs from just 6% of new car sales in 2020 up to 58% in 2026 to date – and a record 67% in the month of September. This year, NEVs’ share of sales has also bounced back from a low of 39% in January, after the full purchase tax exemption that had been in place for more than a decade came to an end in December 2025. The gain in NEV share in 2026 has come amid a slowdown across the wider domestic passenger-car market, which has hit sales of combustion-engine vehicles particularly hard. This September, passenger-car retail sales across all categories declined, with overall sales volumes falling 28% year-on-year. The contraction was led by combustion-engine vehicles, which saw sales fall 43% year-on-year in September and 31% in 2026 to date. However, sales of PHEVs also fell by 36% year-on-year in September and by 26% in 2026 to date. By comparison, BEVs performed better, although sales still declined 8.5% from a year earlier in September and 4% in 2026 to date. As a result, NEV sales overall fell by 17% in September and 13% in 2026 to date. While the market changes in 2026 to date were part
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/chart-a-record-two-thirds-of-car-sales-in-china-are-now-electric-vehicles

--- Editorial (35) ---

* ‘You’re Not Supposed To Be Here’
  New Lines | 23 Oct 15:19 UTC
  Palestinian citizens of Israel face considerable discrimination, with racist rhetoric and marginalization increasing since Oct. 7. Nevertheless, and despite considerable evidence to the contrary, some are convinced that the system can be changed from within. The post ‘You’re Not Supposed To Be Here’ appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: One of the loudest, most vocal — and most surprising — advocates of Israeli policy to emerge since the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks and the start of the Gaza war is a Palestinian journalist who says he is “a proud Zionist.” Haifa-born Yousef Haddad joined the Israeli army after high school and served in a combat unit (Palestinian citizens are exempt from mandatory conscription but may volunteer to serve), then went on to a career as a peripatetic Israel advocate. He has debated on behalf of Israel at the Oxford Union, promoted Israeli policy in Arabic on the Saudi-owned Al Arabiya, visited campuses all over the U.S. and is a regular guest commentator on Israeli television. Now, just ahead of the national election on Oct. 28, he has entered politics as a candidate for a new far-right party called Amcha Yisrael (Your Nation Israel). It is headed by a retired brigadier general, Ofer Winter, a religious Zionist and father of eight who advocates “voluntary emigration” for the Palestinians of Gaza. And yet, for all his pro-Israel posturing, Haddad has not earned the affection of anyone. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu see him as a shill, and Palestinian citizens see him as a native informant. The center-right Jewish majority is happy to celebrate him opportunistically for his propaganda value, but they are suspicious of all Arabs, especially since Oct. 7, so they have declined to embrace him. Religious nationalist Zionists reject him because they are ideologically committed to an ethnically pure Jewish state. * A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes
  New Lines | 23 Oct 10:24 UTC
  A hundred years after its founding, the elite, secular Laique school in Damascus has decided to separate boys and girls for the first time. Syrians are asking what that means for the school, and the future of Syria. The post A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Exactly 100 years ago, a secular French school opened in Damascus with 79 students, in rented premises close to where the current parliament stands on Abid Street. The school was known then as the Laique Institute and is known today as the Al-Hurriya Institute, but is generally referred to as just Laique. Four years later, in 1930, then-Prime Minister Taj al-Din al-Hasani, a turbaned cleric with a reformist agenda, granted the school a plot of land on Baghdad Street, to which it formally relocated in 1932. Laique quickly became one of the most prestigious institutions in Syria, celebrated for its coeducation, the quality of its faculty, and its tennis, volleyball and basketball courts. It also had an impressive library, along with a full-fledged theater that once hosted Egyptian diva Umm Kulthum in 1955. That colorful tapestry was part of a Middle East that imagined itself as part of a broader Arab world with a united identity. Fast forward 100 years, and Laique has become a shadow of its former self. First came 61 years of Baath Party rule that imposed an indoctrinating socialist curriculum on the entire Syrian school system, along with khaki uniforms. Teachers at Laique were hired not because they were good, but because they were Baathists. Then came the 1967 nationalization that ended the school’s autonomy and distorted its institutional identity, renaming it “Mahad al-Huriyya,” or the “Institute of Freedom,” a nod to the language of Baathism, with its three ideological pillars of freedom, pan-Arabism and socialism. The fact that Hafez al-Assad chose Laique for all his children — Bashar included — only hardened the school’s Baathism. When Assad’s eldest son and chosen successor, Bassel al-Assad, died in 1994, the school was renamed after him. What did not disappear was the memory of what Laique had been, and, more importantly, its alumni network of engineers, doctors, politicians and artists who became the “who’s who” of Syrian society. For decades, “I went to Laique” functioned as a kind of passport, akin to “I went to Brummana High School” in Lebanon: a signal of academic excellence that guaranteed entry into the workforce and came with high expectations of talent and character. A great deal of Syrian history was compressed into a single address. This was a school founded under the French mandate, legitimized by a reformist cleric premier, staffed by French and Syrian teachers, and attended by the children of the urban bourgeoisie. * Traction!
  Aeon | 23 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Tribologists study friction: what happens when things rub together. It’s indispensable, from the wheel to the paths of stars - by Jennifer R Vail Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In conversations, few words catch my attention faster than ‘friction’ – and especially talk of making things frictionless . From the workplace to grocery shopping, our societies seem to be striving to remove friction from everything. The motivation appears to be that decreasing resistance makes life easier and smoother. Having spent a career as a tribologist, studying friction, wear and lubrication scientifically, I have some opinions on this. I’ll confess that, in most of my career, I too have sought to reduce friction, developing new materials, designs or even instrumentation in order to lower friction in a jet engine or reduce emissions from your car. However, I’ve also found myself trying to figure out how to increase friction to remove plaque on teeth. The reality is that friction, despite seeming so antagonistic, is necessary and even productive. The force that resists motion has, since humans first struck a spark from a stone, been the very thing that propels us forward. And this paradox offers a lens through which to reread humanity’s entire technological history. I n order to appreciate all that an understanding of friction has helped us to accomplish, it’s necessary to start at square one and state what friction actually is. We define friction as the force that resists motion, and that means friction is rather ubiquitous. If things are moving, whether they’re sliding around or rolling, there’s friction. Solids, liquids, gases? Doesn’t matter. If there’s movement, friction is there to resist it. Unfortunately, because friction’s job is to resist, we’ve developed an aversion to it as a nuisance. But it’s a very important force, and we’ve thrived by expanding our understanding of it. By the 1960s, after millennia of evolving with friction, we finally accepted that friction is such a critical part of our world that a scientific field of study needed to be devoted to it: tribology. Tribologists like me study this force in all its forms. Tribology is the science of interacting surfaces in relative motion, concerned with the wear of materials, friction and lubrication. Basically, tribology is the specialisation of things rubbing against each other. You may raise an eyebrow at a field of science focused on this, but when you pause to consider how much motion is going on inside us, around us and throughout the Universe, you can appreciate that tribology is vast. More than anyone, tribologists know the problems friction can cause. But they also know the ac
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-a-world-without-friction-is-a-world-without-progress

We Saudis find ourselves in a unique position, different from that of the other parties involved. But that sense of stability has now faded, and concerns over domestic issues have intensified with the war, despite the crown prince’s reforms. Another told me that “we’ve gotten used to hearing Houthi missiles.” Perhaps those were not missiles but the sounds of drones. I don’t know, and I don’t think the person I spoke to cares about the difference. The young children in this person’s family are excited because school has been suspended, but that does not change the fact that there is a war going on, and that hearing drones and explosions has become normalized for many Saudis. What is even more troubling is that excessive nationalism has taken hold. Although Saudi Arabia is home to a large population of foreign workers and residents, whose numbers are close to those of Saudi citizens, the current crisis has also brought unfair treatment toward some foreigners. Many Saudis are demanding that foreigners, most of whom are in the country simply to earn a living, publicly express their opinions and show support for the kingdom. The late Middle Ages was a tough time to be alive, between the Black Death, various crusades and holy wars, and famine—not to mention the labor conditions of silver mining. Kutná Hora was also a popular place to die, and all the villagers wanted to spend eternity at the medieval Sedlec Abbey. After one of the crusades, an abbot had brought back a handful of soil, supposedly from Golgotha, and strewn it on the abbey grounds. Burial in that soil was believed to grant quicker access to heaven, which led to an overcrowded cemetery. The solution? Build a subterranean chapel to house the remains. And then a half-blind monk, in the wake of a terrible plague, exhumed the victims and used them to redecorate. An ossuary is any repository for human remains, but it’s also the term for a holy site that serves this function. Another name for this kind of church is charnel house, which to me evokes the image of an abattoir.   FULL TEXT: After ChatGPT was released in November 2022, the tech industry was quick to herald the chatbot as the marker of a new wave of technological progress. As companies pushed to create more powerful AI models, the water demand of their ambitions took a while to become clear. In April 2023, a research paper out of the University of California estimated that training the large language model behind the initial version of ChatGPT had required 185,000 gallons of water. For every extended conversation someone had with the chatbot or every time they asked it to write an email, they were effectively pouring a bottle of water down the drain. That statistic got a lot of play when it was released. It became an early warning sign of what was coming as companies pushed chatbots as a universal technology that could replace psychologists, educators , lawyers, and even people’s friends or partners . As public concern grew, Google and OpenAI came out with their own figures. The companies suggested that querying one of their chatbots used mere drops of water. Independent researchers were not impressed, arguing the companies were taking a narrow view of the water use necessary to power and cool increasingly monstrous data centers. Those water demands can be quite significant too. A hyperscale data center can suck up between 1 and 5 million gallons of water a day, comparable to the amount used by a city of 10,000 to 50,000 people — depending on the part of the world we’re talking about. Consider how these companies often cluster data centers together and it becomes obvious what a challenge it can be to supply them. Data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. The water demands of individual data centers are also continuing to grow. It’s not just that they need more water for cooling as they become larger and contain more servers. The graphics processing units (GPUs) that power generative AI are much more resource-intensive than the chips and storage devices that traditionally filled data center racks. GPUs run harder and throw off more heat , meaning they also need more energy or water to keep them cool. Data centers cannot just use any kind of water either; it has to be clean enough to ensure the equipment and pipes are not damaged. Tapping into public drinking water is both easier and cheaper than paying the cost of treatment themselves. That means data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply.   FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, I surfaced from sleep to a strange noise, a loud, human singing reverberating through our bedroom ceiling, then a low, almost animal groaning. I am 42, and I did not expect to end up here. Like most of my peers, I was handed a vision of a home worth striving for with far, far fewer people in it and, to begin with, I followed that script. A middle-class 1980s child, I did well at school, moved away from my home county dormitory town for university, and never went back. London, jobs at the BBC, running a think tank all followed. I lived with good friends, and juggled seeing others between our clashing personal schedules. Then people began to pair up and procreate. I met someone, followed suit, and we clawed our way onto the very bottom of the housing ladder. It was a lovely life. A bit relentless, perhaps, a bit lonely around the edges, too, as the schedules got ever harder to tessellate, the work and care burdens heavier. But we had done it. Our little dyad, with dependants, was standing on its own feet, even if something felt decidedly unstable underneath. More space, more privacy, more control – all are sold as natural, unquestioned goods to be sought in the unconscious script that most of us carry. For the nuclear family, they are tokens of success and maturity, whereas forgoing these ‘goods’ and living with people you are neither shagging nor parenting has an association with low-status, studenty seasons.   Longreads | 20 Oct 16:12 UTC
  "The works of the British art critic John Berger offer the antidote to our age of infinite scrolling."
  FULL TEXT: John Berger spent a meaningful portion of his life thinking, with precision and care, about how to look at the world—an effort he detailed in numerous essays and books, among them Ways of Seeing and About Looking . “Reading them, you often feel you’re standing next to him in the museum or even looking through his eyes at a painting or a sculpture,” writes A.O. Scott. “You pay attention to his attention.” Scott, an attentive reader himself , brings Berger’s lifelong project into our famously distractable era; his consideration of Berger is a primer for observation and study, a gentle nudge to focus, lest we lose sight of something essential. Right now, we’re in the midst of an attention crisis, individually and collectively snared in an infinite scroll of disruption and distraction. Berger saw this crisis coming and foresaw its consequences. In 1978, writing about photography and its impact on the modern world, he argued that the dominance of images “creates an eternal present of immediate expectation: Memory ceases to be necessary or desirable. With the loss of memory, the continuities of meaning and judgment are also lost to us.” That diagnosis seems even truer today, as the functions of remembering, judging and interpreting are increasingly offloaded from human minds and communities into the ether of social media feeds and artificial intelligence. We’re in danger of forgetting how to look. “A conversation with the NYT critic on his popular close reads of poems.” “Nature writing after everyone stopped paying attention.” “Today, photo geeks the world over consider Paul Kwilecki a master of the documentary form.”

* How intuitions work
  Aeon | 20 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Just as seeing a tree gives you reason to believe it’s there, sensing that a stranger is creepy gives you reason to walk away - by Marc A Moffett Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: At the Campidoglio, Rome, 1984. Photo by Richard Kalvar/Magnum When people say such things, they are not typically drawing conclusions from a chain of reasoning. The person who avoids getting in the stranger’s car is not thinking: ‘He displayed these three behavioural cues and, therefore, he poses a risk.’ The email isn’t flagged by going through an explicit checklist. The deal doesn’t announce its flaws through a formal audit. In each case, something is simply presented as having a certain character: the man as creepy, the email as fraudulent, the deal as suspect. And yet, acting on these intuitions seems not merely excusable but entirely reasonable . If the man really does strike you as creepy, staying out of his car was probably the smart call – even if you can’t say why. Despite this, ‘vibes’ and ‘intuitions’ are often dismissed both in daily life and in academic enquiry as irrational or merely heuristic devices. And while we cannot always trust such reactions, they are central, indeed indispensable, to our ability to rationally navigate the world. Philosophers, however, have done surprisingly little to illuminate the rational credentials of these ordinary kinds of intuitions. Instead, they have focused the bulk of their attention on a much more rarefied kind of intuition called rational intuition , which is taken to function primarily in the a priori disciplines of philosophy, logic and mathematics. My objective is to explain why our everyday intuitive reactions can have such rational force. The key is to see that intuition works in much the same way as perception, in that both can directly present something to us as actual. Once we understand what gives perception this feature, we can see why intuitions might involve exercising the same basic kind of cognitive capacity. A standard way of characterising rational intuition is in terms of seemings , by analogy with perception. Just as it perceptually seems to you that there is a car in the garage, so it can intellectually seem to you that 2 + 2 = 4 – the former presenting a physical fact, the latter a conceptual one. But rational intuition and the everyday intuition at work when you decline to get in a stranger’s car are not as far apart as this rarefied example suggests: the philosopher’s intuition is, at bottom, a highly specialised version of the same basic capacity. To draw these two capacities together, let’s consider a favourite pastime of philosophers: the thought experiment. Philosophers have a 
  https://aeon.co/essays/sometimes-you-just-know-somethings-off-but-how

Officials agreed to continue talks on an initiative that aims to guarantee a halt to “hostile actions” against Saudi Arabia and other Arab countries, ensuring safety and freedom of maritime routes and initiating a process to reach a political solution to the crisis. Bakry added that, next Monday, the parliament will take a position that reaffirms Egypt’s “firm and principled” solidarity with Saudi Arabia. It would be years before I saw it as a source of political conviction; something that once split my family’s loyalty between their hungry children and a revolutionary cause.” In this essay at Orion , Victoria Blanco writes about her Abuelita, whose family had owned a burrería in downtown Juárez during the Mexican Revolution, and how she worked hard there through childhood, making burritos for Pancho Villa’s troops—an army fighting for a better life for the next generation. “The food we ate was built for movement,” she writes, “the same way we were.” Blanco reflects on the burrito as a symbol resistance, the borderland, and belonging. I found the unspoken parts of Abuelita’s childhood when I was working in a university archive as a student, examining photographs no one in my family had ever seen. One image stopped me completely: a rooftop terrace at the Paso del Norte hotel, where well-dressed Americans held cocktails and binoculars, watching the war across the river as though it were a form of entertainment. On the other side of that same river—a river with two names, Río Bravo on one bank, Rio Grande on the other—men were falling from gunfire while children went without food so that revolutionaries could have the sustenance they needed to keep fighting. I sat with that photograph for a long time, thinking about my abuelita, a girl somewhere in that city while Americans sipped cocktails and watched her world from a hotel roof. “The quiet medicine of small jobs, finished before dark.” “A conversation about finding community in the aquatic.” “Does the ivory-billed woodpecker still exist?” “The Blackbyrds’ ode to DC inspired a new generation of artists.” “Geography, capitalism, and America by bus.” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014.   FULL TEXT: Gen. Abdulrahman al-Mahrami, known as Abu Zaraa, is one of the most visible figures in Yemen’s military and media landscape today. Al-Mahrami’s involvement is not simply a matter of his role as commander of the Southern Giants Brigades, or his seat on the Presidential Leadership Council (PLC), Yemen’s internationally recognized government. It is also the region where he made his name nine years ago in a previous fight to drive the Houthis out. Born in 1980 in the Rasad-Yafaa district in southern Yemen, al-Mahrami is fighting to protect his most significant military achievement, one that underpinned his subsequent rise as both a military and political figure. Two other commanders — Brig. Gen. Hamdi Shukri al-Subaihi and Maj. Gen. Abdulrahman al-Lahji — share both his record on the west coast and his religious background as a Salafist, the conservative brand of Sunni Islam that models itself on the behavior of the Prophet Muhammad’s earliest companions. In the first operation to wrest the region from the Houthis, between January 2017 and November 2018, the United Arab Emirates oversaw the campaign. Saudi Arabia is overseeing the current one, with all three men once again involved. Together, they have come to encapsulate the battle for Yemen as it currently stands, with its shifting battlefronts and the carnage it is now wreaking in both Yemen itself and Saudi Arabia. Understanding these men prosecuting the conflict on the ground provides a unique and vital perspective on its dynamics and evolution. For Brig. Gen. Hamdi Shukri al-Subaihi, the first campaign back in 2018 was a watershed, completing his transformation from preacher into military commander, along with a range of security and social roles. Before the Houthis reached Yemen’s southern governorates in 2015, Shukri was a little-known teacher of Islamic sciences in the Subaiha areas of Lahj governorate. The Houthi offensive against the Salafist movement changed that. The siege of the Dar al-Hadith center in Dammaj in Saada governorate — a battle that lasted over two years and ended in January 2014 with over 200 deaths, the expulsion of its students and then the push into the southern governorates — drove many in that milieu from preaching into fighting.   FULL TEXT: Hosted by Faisal Al Yafai Featuring Anand Giridharadas Produced by Finbar Anderson Listen to and follow The Lede Apple Podcasts | Spotify | Youtube | Podbean Anand Giridharadas remembers the fallout from the May 2023 killing of a young homeless man on a New York city subway train. “From the moment that Daniel Penny put Jordan Neely in a chokehold and killed him on the floor of that F train in New York, it immediately was an American Rorschach test,” he tells Faisal Al Yafai on The Lede. “You were either looking at an act of heroism by a Marine who stood up to defend an endangered public, or you were looking at a white supremacist vigilante in Trump’s America.” The case was widely discussed across the United States, and the trial that followed the killing was followed closely by audiences on both sides of the political spectrum. If you have appropriate levels of care for folks going through homelessness, people who are mentally ill, you probably wouldn’t have had that situation. … In other words, when the doors close, the country still is in that subway car.” Against the backdrop of this compelling allegory for modern America, Giridharadas, an acclaimed writer and former New York Times columnist, set out to write his recently published book, “Man in the Mirror: Hope, Struggle, and Belonging in an American City.” In it, he tells Al Yafai, he has endeavored to avoid pronouncing judgment on the killing itself. “I think this book will do something that’s actually much harder than making an argument. It’s actually very hard to build movements. You have to know deep in your bones and be able to even imagine what it’s like to be your neighbors.” On a previous episode of The Lede’s “American Experiment” series, Giridharadas and Al Yafai discussed how much of contemporary America feels abandoned by traditional political parties. * A vocabulary for the future: nature
  Aeon | 19 Oct 10:01 UTC
  For the philosopher Bruno Latour, seeing anything ‘natural’ as good and the ‘artificial’ as bad is perversely human-centric - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: For the French philosopher Bruno Latour , ‘nature’ can’t exist in isolation, but only in relation to the ‘artificial’ or the ‘human-made’. In drawing a solid line between these two domains, he argues, we’re making a moral judgment rather than an ontological one: nature as good, its opposite as bad. Thinking outside that binary, he suggests, means rethinking the world not only as something acted upon by human agents, but as populated by other beings with agency of their own. Paired with visuals by the Barcelona-born, London-based filmmaker Gerard Ortín Castellví, this video gives shape to the divide Latour questions, showing the boundary between the natural world and the human one collapsing, to suggest the two are far more entangled than we tend to assume. This video is part of the series A Vocabulary for the Future , produced by the Centre de Cultura Contemporània de Barcelona. As many as 1.8 million people use the app daily. Meta aspires to a global launch. “[We] are quite excited to bring personal agents to billions of people around the world,” Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer, told the Financial Times . Some startups are already building agents in markets that Big Tech hopes to reach. For instance, South African startup ManyHands offers Manny , a personal assistant designed to order groceries, book appointments and make payments through WhatsApp. But adapting agents to local conditions has its challenges. Anton Moulder, ManyHands’ founder, told Rest of World that getting the AI assistant to converse in all of South Africa’s 11 official spoken languages has been difficult. Rest of World spoke to AI researchers and other experts to understand how these personal agents would perform outside Western nations. English speakers could benefit more from AI agents, which run on top of large language models that underperform in many non-English languages . “Linguistic inclusion is ultimately about power,” Payal Arora, professor of inclusive AI cultures at Utrecht University and the founder of Inclusive AI Lab, told Rest of World . Meta has said that expanding Muse internationally would require support for local languages and integration with local services. AI agents would also need to understand the local culture , including the unique ways people communicate and bargain. “In many parts of the Global South, meaning is deeply contextual, shaped by family, community, religion, local institutions, and informal social networks,” Arora said. Without that understanding, agents might impose Western assumptions on their users, Usha Raman, an independent researcher on AI policy and digital culture, told Rest of World. “The danger then is that existing forms of cultural and economic hegemony continue and the rest of the world is forced to adapt,” she said. * Horrible, unmanly, indispensable
  Aeon | 19 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Renaissance Italians saw the gun as the Devil’s work. But soon they embraced this unchivalrous – and necessary – weapon - by Catherine Fletcher Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from The Battle of Pavia, 1525 ( c 1529) by Robert Heller. Courtesy the Nationalmuseum, Sweden /Wikipedia If you were asked to think of a gun owner, then Lorenzo de’ Medici – the celebrated patron of Renaissance art – would probably not be the first person to come to mind. In fact, however, Lorenzo ‘the Magnificent’ owned five portable firearms, which are listed in his 1492 postmortem inventory. Guns and gunpowder were controversial. In art and literature, they were labelled the Devil’s work, and were perceived as fundamentally unchivalrous. Yet within half a century, by the early months of 1539, Lorenzo’s descendant Duke Cosimo de’ Medici had 91 firearms in his Florentine storerooms. Somehow, in the courts of late-Renaissance Italy, the gun had become an acceptable, even valued, weapon. Beyond the palazzi of the peninsula’s towns and cities, guns were also making inroads into more ordinary lives. There were no standing armies in the 16th-century Italian states. Their wars were fought through temporary mobilisations, and with considerable help from companies of mercenaries. Yet as the 1506 provision that established the Florentine militia made clear, ‘little hope’ could be placed ‘in foreign and hired arms’. Far better to establish a new magistracy with ‘full authority and power’ to enlist men from Florentine territory, at least 10,000 of them. One in 10 of these men were to be equipped with firearms, and 500 handguns ( schioppetti ) should be kept in the Palazzo della Signoria (the city hall, now known as the Palazzo Vecchio) along with ‘such money as should be necessary for the schioppetti ’, presumably to purchase gunpowder and shot. Three or four companies ( bandiere ) entirely of schioppettieri might also be established. This militia, most often noted for the role of Niccolò Machiavelli in its foundation, was to be organised after the fashion of the Germans, in whose cities there was a centuries-old tradition of civic defence and a duty on every male citizen to maintain suitable arms for the purpose. In fact, one of the Florentine militia’s early constables was German (a Giovanni Tedesco); another (Giliberto Spagnolo) came from Spain, a country making the running in the use of small arms on the battlefield. Giovanni Tedesco arranged target practice for his company, which from 1508 was extended more generally among the militia, with exercises taking place on a monthly basis. M ilitias were not new but, by the 1490s, large-scale foreign military
